    Label idle $=1$, busy $=2$: $p_{11}=0.6$, $p_{12}=0.4$, $p_{21}=0.1$, $p_{22}=0.9$. Balance for state 1: $\pi_1=0.6\pi_1+0.1\pi_2\Rightarrow0.4\pi_1=0.1\pi_2\Rightarrow\pi_2=4\pi_1$. Normalization: $\pi_1+4\pi_1=1$, so $\pi_1=1/5=0.2$ and $\pi_2=4/5=0.8$.

    Revenue per minute $=3\pi_2=3(0.8)=\$2.40$ by the state-cost formula. Setup cost per minute $=1\cdot\pi_1p_{12}=1\cdot(0.2)(0.4)=\$0.08$ by the transition-cost formula. Long-run profit $=2.40-0.08=\$2.32$ per minute. The two formulas cannot be mixed up: revenue is attached to states, setup to transitions.

    3.4 Birth–death chains and the cut (local balance) equations

    Definition (birth–death chain; L17 slide 8, B&T §7.3 p. 358 and its Figure 7.14). A chain on states $\{0,1,\dots,m\}$ is a birth–death chain if in one step it can only move to an adjacent state or stay put: $$p_{i,i+1}=p_i\ \ (\text{a "birth"}),\qquad p_{i,i-1}=q_i\ \ (\text{a "death"}),\qquad p_{ii}=1-p_i-q_i,$$ with the boundary conventions $q_0=0$ and $p_m=0$ (so $p_{00}=1-p_0$ and $p_{mm}=1-q_m$), and all other $p_{ij}=0$. The state $i$ is typically a population size, a queue length, or a number of busy servers, so $p_i$ is the chance it goes up by one this period and $q_i$ the chance it goes down.
    Fig. 3.5 — The birth–death chain and the cut argument (redrawn from L17 slide 8, which draws the same dashed cut; compare B&T §7.3, its Figure 7.14). Every path from the left block to the right block must use the single arc $i\to i+1$, and every path back must use $i+1\to i$ — which is what forces the two frequencies to be equal.

    The cut equation, derived

    L17 slide 8 asserts the cut equation from the "frequencies must balance" picture. That picture is correct and worth keeping (see below), but the equation also follows from the ordinary balance equations by a short induction, which is what the slide's [DERIVATION GAP] asks for. Here it is.

    Claim. For a birth–death chain, the steady-state probabilities satisfy the local balance (or cut) equations $$\pi_i\,p_i=\pi_{i+1}\,q_{i+1},\qquad i=0,1,\dots,m-1 .$$

    Base case $i=0$. The balance equation for state 0 collects the ways of arriving in 0: from 0 itself (probability $p_{00}=1-p_0$) and from 1 (probability $q_1$). No other state can reach 0 in one step. So

    $$\pi_0=\pi_0(1-p_0)+\pi_1q_1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \pi_0-\pi_0+\pi_0p_0=\pi_1q_1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \pi_0p_0=\pi_1q_1 . \checkmark$$

    Inductive step. Suppose $\pi_{i-1}p_{i-1}=\pi_iq_i$ holds for some $i\ge1$; we prove $\pi_ip_i=\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}$. The balance equation for state $i$ has exactly three incoming terms — from $i-1$ by a birth, from $i$ by staying, from $i+1$ by a death:

    $$\pi_i=\pi_{i-1}p_{i-1}+\pi_i\,(1-p_i-q_i)+\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}.$$

    Expand the middle term and cancel $\pi_i$ from both sides:

    $$ \begin{aligned} \pi_i&=\pi_{i-1}p_{i-1}+\pi_i-\pi_ip_i-\pi_iq_i+\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}\\ 0&=\pi_{i-1}p_{i-1}-\pi_iq_i-\pi_ip_i+\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1} &&\text{subtract }\pi_i\text{ from both sides}\\ 0&=\underbrace{(\pi_{i-1}p_{i-1}-\pi_iq_i)}_{=\,0\ \text{by the inductive hypothesis}} \;-\;\pi_ip_i+\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}\\ \pi_ip_i&=\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}. \checkmark \end{aligned} $$

    By induction the cut equation holds for every $i$ from $0$ to $m-1$. (The balance equation for the top state $m$ is then automatically satisfied — it is the redundant one of §3.1.)

    Interpretation (why the picture is the proof). Draw a vertical line between states $i$ and $i+1$, splitting the state space into the left block $\{0,\dots,i\}$ and the right block $\{i+1,\dots,m\}$ (Fig. 3.5). Because the chain moves only between neighbors, the only way to get from left to right is the single transition $i\to i+1$, whose long-run frequency is $\pi_ip_i$; the only way back is $i+1\to i$, with frequency $\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}$. In any long stretch of $n$ steps these crossings must alternate — you cannot cross left-to-right twice in a row without crossing back in between — so the two counts differ by at most 1, and after dividing by $n$ and letting $n\to\infty$ the frequencies are equal. This is the argument on L17 slide 8; the induction above is the same statement in algebra.
    Gotcha. Local balance is not a general fact about Markov chains. It is a special feature of chains whose transition graph is a line (or, more generally, a tree, or any chain that is "reversible"). B&T Problem 7.25 asks for a chain with a single aperiodic recurrent class whose $\pi$ does not satisfy $\pi_ip_{ij}=\pi_jp_{ji}$ — a three-state cycle $1\to2\to3\to1$ with a little back-flow does the job: probability circulates around the loop, so individual pairs are not in balance even though every state is. If your chain has a $1\to2\to3\to1$ shortcut, you must solve the full linear system.

    Telescoping to a closed form

    The cut equations solve themselves. Rearranged, $\pi_{i+1}=\pi_i\,p_i/q_{i+1}$, so starting at $\pi_0$ and stepping up:

    $$ \pi_1=\pi_0\frac{p_0}{q_1},\qquad \pi_2=\pi_1\frac{p_1}{q_2}=\pi_0\frac{p_0p_1}{q_1q_2},\qquad\dots,\qquad \boxed{\ \pi_i=\pi_0\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{p_{k-1}}{q_k}\ },\quad i=0,1,\dots,m, $$

    with the empty product for $i=0$ read as $1$. Then $\pi_0$ is fixed by normalization:

    $$\pi_0\left[1+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{p_{k-1}}{q_k}\right]=1 .$$

    An $m+1$-dimensional linear system has become a product and one division. That is the entire reason birth–death chains are a named special case.

    Practice 3.7. A birth–death chain on $\{0,1,2\}$ has $p_0=0.5$, $p_1=0.4$, $q_1=0.3$, $q_2=0.6$. Find $\pi$ by local balance, then verify with the ordinary balance equation for state 1.
    Solution

    Self-loop probabilities are $p_{00}=1-0.5=0.5$, $p_{11}=1-0.4-0.3=0.3$, $p_{22}=1-0.6=0.4$; all are in $[0,1]$, so the chain is legal. Local balance: $$\pi_1=\pi_0\frac{p_0}{q_1}=\pi_0\frac{0.5}{0.3}=\frac{5}{3}\pi_0,\qquad \pi_2=\pi_1\frac{p_1}{q_2}=\frac{5}{3}\pi_0\cdot\frac{0.4}{0.6}=\frac{5}{3}\cdot\frac{2}{3}\pi_0 =\frac{10}{9}\pi_0 .$$ Normalize: $\pi_0\left(1+\frac53+\frac{10}{9}\right)=\pi_0\cdot\frac{9+15+10}{9} =\frac{34}{9}\pi_0=1$, so $$\pi_0=\frac{9}{34}\approx0.264706,\quad \pi_1=\frac{15}{34}\approx0.441176,\quad \pi_2=\frac{10}{34}\approx0.294118 .$$ Check with the balance equation for state 1: $\pi_0p_0+\pi_1p_{11}+\pi_2q_2 =\frac{9}{34}(0.5)+\frac{15}{34}(0.3)+\frac{10}{34}(0.6) =\frac{4.5+4.5+6}{34}=\frac{15}{34}=\pi_1$ ✓.

    3.5 The queue: constant $p$ and $q$, the load factor $\rho$, and what happens as $\rho\to1$

    Specialize to the case L17 slide 8 highlights and B&T Example 7.9 models: a buffer holding at most $m$ packets, where in each period at most one thing happens — an arrival with probability $p$, a departure with probability $q$, nothing with the remaining probability. Then $p_i=p$ for $i<m$ and $q_i=q$ for $i>0$, and every ratio in the telescoping product is the same number.

    Definition (load factor). $\displaystyle\rho=\frac{p}{q}$, the ratio of the arrival probability to the departure probability. It is dimensionless: the average number of arrivals per departure. $\rho<1$ means work drains faster than it arrives; $\rho>1$ means the opposite.

    With $p_{k-1}/q_k=p/q=\rho$ for every $k$, the product collapses:

    $$\pi_{i+1}=\pi_i\frac{p}{q}=\pi_i\rho \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \pi_i=\pi_0\,\rho^{\,i},\qquad i=0,1,\dots,m,$$

    which is the formula printed on L17 slide 8. Normalization needs the finite geometric sum:

    $$1=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\pi_i=\pi_0\sum_{i=0}^{m}\rho^{\,i} =\begin{cases}\pi_0\dfrac{1-\rho^{\,m+1}}{1-\rho},&\rho\neq1,\\[8pt] \pi_0\,(m+1),&\rho=1,\end{cases}$$

    using $\sum_{i=0}^{m}x^i=(1-x^{m+1})/(1-x)$ for $x\neq1$ (and $m+1$ equal terms when $x=1$). Therefore

    $$\boxed{\ \pi_0=\frac{1-\rho}{1-\rho^{\,m+1}}\ (\rho\neq1),\qquad \pi_0=\frac{1}{m+1}\ (\rho=1), \qquad \pi_i=\pi_0\rho^{\,i}.\ }$$

    Three sanity checks, all recomputed in computes/g5_s3.py section D by building the full $(m{+}1)\times(m{+}1)$ transition matrix and solving $\pi=\pi P$ with numpy — the closed form and the linear solve agree to at most $1.7\times10^{-15}$ in every case, and the local-balance residual $\max_i|\pi_ip-\pi_{i+1}q|$ is below $3\times10^{-16}$:

    case$p$$q$$\rho$$m$$\pi_0$$\pi_m$$\mathbb{E}[X]$
    light load0.200.400.5100.5002440.0004890.994626
    heavy load0.240.300.8100.2187940.0234932.966314
    critical0.300.301.0100.0909090.0909095.000000
    overload0.450.301.5100.0058480.3372328.128659
    Fig. 3.6 — Steady-state distribution of the queue length for three load factors, buffer $m=10$ (L17 slide 8's formula $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^{\,i}$). Under-loaded: geometric decay, mostly empty. Critically loaded: exactly uniform. Over-loaded: geometric growth, mostly full.
    Interpretation (the shape is the whole story). $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^{\,i}$ is a geometric sequence, so the plot is a straight line on a log axis with slope $\log\rho$. If $\rho<1$ the line slopes down and the system is usually near-empty; if $\rho=1$ the line is flat and every occupancy is equally likely — a system with no preferred operating point; if $\rho>1$ the line slopes up and the system is usually near-full, i.e. the buffer is the only thing keeping the queue finite.

    The infinite buffer, $\rho<1$

    L17 slide 8 then takes $p<q$ (so $\rho<1$) and $m\approx\infty$ and asserts $\pi_0=1-\rho$ and $\mathbb{E}[X_n]=\rho/(1-\rho)$, with both derivations left off the slide. Here they are.

    The normalization. With $\rho<1$ we have $\rho^{\,m+1}\to0$ as $m\to\infty$, so

    $$\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\pi_i=\pi_0\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\rho^{\,i}=\frac{\pi_0}{1-\rho}=1 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \pi_0=1-\rho,$$

    using the infinite geometric series $\sum_{i\ge0}\rho^i=1/(1-\rho)$, which converges precisely because $\rho<1$. Hence

    $$\pi_i=(1-\rho)\rho^{\,i},\qquad i=0,1,2,\dots$$

    The mean. Two routes to the same answer; take whichever you find faster.

    Route 1 (recognize the PMF). $\pi_i=(1-\rho)\rho^i$ for $i=0,1,2,\dots$ is the PMF of a shifted geometric random variable: if $Y$ is geometric with parameter $1-\rho$ on $\{1,2,\dots\}$, i.e. $p_Y(y)=(1-\rho)\rho^{\,y-1}$, then $X=Y-1$ has exactly this PMF. From G2, $\mathbb{E}[Y]=1/(1-\rho)$, so

    $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[Y]-1=\frac{1}{1-\rho}-1=\frac{1-(1-\rho)}{1-\rho}=\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}.$$

    Route 2 (sum directly). Start from $\sum_{i\ge0}\rho^i=\frac{1}{1-\rho}$ and differentiate both sides with respect to $\rho$ (legal inside the radius of convergence):

    $$\sum_{i\ge1}i\,\rho^{\,i-1}=\frac{1}{(1-\rho)^2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \sum_{i\ge0}i\,\rho^{\,i}=\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)^2}\ \ \text{(multiply by }\rho).$$

    Therefore

    $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i\ge0}i\,\pi_i=(1-\rho)\sum_{i\ge0}i\rho^{\,i} =(1-\rho)\cdot\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)^2}=\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}. \checkmark$$

    Both [DERIVATION GAP]s on L17 slide 8 are now closed. The formula is checked numerically in computes/g5_s3.py section D by truncating the geometric distribution at $4000$ states: the largest discrepancy over $\rho\in\{0.2,0.5,0.8,0.9,0.95,0.99\}$ is $4.3\times10^{-14}$.

    $\rho$0.20.50.80.90.950.99
    $\pi_0=1-\rho$0.8000.5000.2000.1000.0500.010
    $\mathbb{E}[X]=\rho/(1-\rho)$0.251.004.009.0019.0099.00
    Fig. 3.7 — The load blow-up (L17 slide 8, $\mathbb{E}[X_n]=\rho/(1-\rho)$). A finite buffer of size $m$ tracks the infinite-buffer curve until $\rho$ approaches 1, then saturates at $m/2$ and beyond — the queue does not blow up, the losses do.
    Interpretation (why $\rho\to1$ is a cliff, not a slope). Read the table left to right. Going from $\rho=0.5$ to $\rho=0.8$ — a 60% increase in load — costs you 3 extra jobs in the system. Going from $\rho=0.9$ to $\rho=0.99$ — a 10% increase — costs you 90. The function $\rho/(1-\rho)$ has a pole at $\rho=1$: as the server approaches full utilization the expected backlog diverges, and the closer you are to the cliff the more each additional percent costs. This is the reason real systems are engineered to run at 60–80% utilization rather than 99%: the last few percent of "efficiency" are paid for in unbounded delay. Note also the mechanism — what blows up is not the arrival rate (bounded by $q$) but the variability: at $\rho$ near 1 the random walk $X_n$ has almost no restoring drift, so it wanders far before being pulled back.

    $\rho\ge1$: no steady state without a buffer

    If $\rho>1$ the series $\sum_i\rho^i$ diverges, so no choice of $\pi_0>0$ can normalize $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^i$ over infinitely many states: there is no stationary distribution. The finite-$m$ formula still works and shows what goes wrong as the buffer grows. With $p=0.45$, $q=0.30$ ($\rho=1.5$), from computes/g5_s3.py:

    $m$510204080
    $\pi_0$$4.81\times10^{-2}$$5.85\times10^{-3}$$1.00\times10^{-4}$$3.02\times10^{-8}$$2.73\times10^{-15}$
    $\pi_m$0.3654140.3372320.3334000.3333330.333333
    $\mathbb{E}[X]$3.5778.12918.00438.00078.000

    Every fixed state's probability decays to $0$ as $m\to\infty$ (B&T p. 361 makes exactly this point) while $\pi_m\to1-1/\rho=1-2/3=1/3$ piles up at the ceiling and $\mathbb{E}[X]\approx m-2$ tracks the buffer. In the infinite-buffer limit the chain visits every particular state only finitely often — the queue grows without bound. The case $\rho=1$ is the knife's edge: $\pi_i=1/(m+1)$ for every $i$, uniform, and $\mathbb{E}[X]=m/2$, so it too diverges with the buffer, just linearly rather than by pile-up.

    Practice 3.8. A packet buffer holds at most $m=4$ packets. In each slot an arrival occurs with probability $p=0.3$ and a departure with probability $q=0.5$. Find $\pi$ exactly and the expected number in the buffer.
    Solution

    $\rho=p/q=0.3/0.5=0.6=3/5$. Local balance gives $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^i$, and normalization needs $$\sum_{i=0}^{4}\left(\tfrac35\right)^i=1+\tfrac35+\tfrac9{25}+\tfrac{27}{125}+\tfrac{81}{625} =\frac{625+375+225+135+81}{625}=\frac{1441}{625}=2.3056 .$$ Hence $\pi_0=\dfrac{625}{1441}=0.433727$, and $$\pi=(0.433727,\;0.260236,\;0.156142,\;0.093685,\;0.056211),$$ matching computes/g5_s3.py section F. Mean: $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i=0}^4 i\pi_i=1.078418$ packets. For comparison the infinite-buffer value would be $\rho/(1-\rho)=0.6/0.4=1.5$; the 4-packet ceiling has cut the mean by about 28%, because the probability mass that an unbounded buffer would put on states $\ge5$ — a fraction $\rho^5=0.6^5=0.07776$ of the time, by the tail formula of Practice 3.9 — has been squeezed back down into states $0$ through $4$.

    Practice 3.9. An infinite-buffer queue must satisfy $\mathbb{E}[X]\le4$. What is the largest tolerable load factor, and at that load what fraction of time is the queue empty and what is $\mathbf{P}(X\ge5)$?
    Solution

    Solve $\rho/(1-\rho)=4$: $\rho=4-4\rho$, so $5\rho=4$ and $\rho=0.8$. That is the largest tolerable value, since $\rho/(1-\rho)$ is increasing in $\rho$ (its derivative $1/(1-\rho)^2$ is positive).

    Empty fraction: $\pi_0=1-\rho=0.2$, i.e. 20% of periods. Tail: since $\pi_i=(1-\rho)\rho^i$, $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge5)=\sum_{i\ge5}(1-\rho)\rho^i=(1-\rho)\rho^5\sum_{j\ge0}\rho^j =(1-\rho)\rho^5\cdot\frac{1}{1-\rho}=\rho^5=0.8^5=0.32768 .$$ (The general fact worth remembering: $\mathbf{P}(X\ge k)=\rho^k$, a clean geometric tail.) At $\rho=0.9$ the same tail would be $0.9^5=0.59049$ — computed in computes/g5_s3.py section F — so nudging the load up by an eighth nearly doubles the chance of a deep backlog.

    3.6 The phone company: an inhomogeneous birth–death chain (L18 slide 4)

    Example 3.5 (L18 slide 4 — probability of blocked phone calls; derived in full). Calls originate as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$. Each call's duration is exponential with parameter $\mu$, independently of everything else. The exchange has $B$ lines; a call that arrives when all $B$ are busy is blocked (lost, not queued). What fraction of calls is blocked?

    Step 1 — discretize time. Chop time into slots of small length $\delta$ and let $X_n$ be the number of calls in progress at the end of slot $n$; the state space is $\{0,1,\dots,B\}$. From G4 §3, the defining small-interval property of a Poisson process gives

    $$\mathbf{P}(\text{one arrival in a slot})=\lambda\delta+o(\delta),\qquad \mathbf{P}(\text{two or more arrivals})=o(\delta).$$

    For departures: a call in progress has an exponential duration, which is memoryless (G3), so regardless of how long it has already been running it ends during the next $\delta$ with probability $\mu\delta+o(\delta)$. If $i$ calls are in progress, they are independent, so the probability that exactly one of them ends is $i\mu\delta+o(\delta)$ (the $i$ ways of choosing which one, each of probability $\mu\delta$, minus overlaps of order $\delta^2$). Dropping all $o(\delta)$ terms — legitimate in the limit $\delta\to0$ — we get a birth–death chain with

    $$p_i=\lambda\delta\ \ (0\le i<B),\qquad q_i=i\mu\delta\ \ (1\le i\le B),\qquad p_{ii}=1-\lambda\delta-i\mu\delta .$$

    This closes the first half of the L18 slide-4 [DERIVATION GAP]. Note the structure: the birth rate is constant (arrivals do not care how busy you are), while the death rate is proportional to the state (the more calls in progress, the more of them can finish).

    Fig. 3.8 — The discretized phone-exchange chain (redrawn from L18 slide 4). Births happen at the constant rate $\lambda\delta$; deaths from state $i$ at rate $i\mu\delta$, because any one of the $i$ calls in progress may end.

    Step 2 — local balance. The chain is birth–death, so §3.4's cut equation applies across the cut between $i-1$ and $i$:

    $$\pi_{i-1}\,(\lambda\delta)=\pi_i\,(i\mu\delta) \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \lambda\,\pi_{i-1}=i\mu\,\pi_i,\qquad i=1,\dots,B,$$

    which is the equation printed on the slide. The slot length $\delta$ appears on both sides and cancels — the answer does not depend on how finely we chopped time, which is exactly what we needed for the discretization to be an honest model of the continuous-time system.

    Step 3 — telescope. Rearranging, $\pi_i=\pi_{i-1}\lambda/(i\mu)$. Applying this repeatedly from $\pi_0$:

    $$ \pi_1=\pi_0\frac{\lambda}{1\mu},\qquad \pi_2=\pi_1\frac{\lambda}{2\mu}=\pi_0\frac{\lambda^2}{2\mu^2},\qquad \pi_3=\pi_2\frac{\lambda}{3\mu}=\pi_0\frac{\lambda^3}{3\cdot2\,\mu^3},\ \dots $$ $$\pi_i=\pi_0\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{\lambda}{k\mu} =\pi_0\frac{\lambda^i}{\mu^i}\cdot\frac{1}{1\cdot2\cdots i} =\pi_0\frac{\lambda^i}{\mu^i\,i!},\qquad i=0,1,\dots,B,$$

    the formula on the slide (second half of the [DERIVATION GAP]: the product $\prod_k 1/k$ is what produces the factorial).

    Step 4 — normalize.

    $$1=\sum_{i=0}^{B}\pi_i=\pi_0\sum_{i=0}^{B}\frac{\lambda^i}{\mu^i\,i!} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \pi_0=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{B}\frac{\lambda^i}{\mu^i\,i!}\right)^{-1}.$$
    Interpretation (a truncated Poisson). Write $a=\lambda/\mu$, the offered load, measured in erlangs: it is the mean number of calls that would be in progress if there were unlimited lines (arrivals per unit time $\times$ mean duration). Then $\pi_i\propto a^i/i!$, which is the Poisson PMF with parameter $a$ up to the normalizing constant — the same distribution, restricted to $\{0,\dots,B\}$ and renormalized. If there were unlimited lines the normalizing constant would be $e^{-a}$ and the number of calls in progress would be exactly Poisson with parameter $a$ — the continuous-time echo of G4's Poisson results. The finite $B$ chops off the tail, and the chopped-off mass is what turns into blocking.

    Step 5 — read off the blocking probability. A call is blocked exactly when it arrives to find the system in state $B$. Because arrivals come from a Poisson process, they are equally likely to fall at any instant and are independent of the current state, so the fraction of arriving calls that see state $B$ equals the fraction of time the system spends in state $B$ — which is $\pi_B$ by §3.3. Hence

    $$\boxed{\ \mathbf{P}(\text{call blocked})=\pi_B =\frac{a^B/B!}{\sum_{i=0}^{B}a^i/i!},\qquad a=\frac{\lambda}{\mu}.\ }$$

    This is the Erlang-B formula, the oldest formula in telephone engineering (Erlang, 1917). L18 slide 4 stops before naming it; the interpretation is the missing last line of the slide.

    A worked instance. Take $\lambda=30$ calls per minute and a mean call duration of $1/\mu=0.1$ minutes ($\mu=10$ per minute), with $B=4$ lines. Then $a=\lambda/\mu=3$ erlangs, and the unnormalized weights are

    $$1,\quad 3,\quad \frac{3^2}{2}=4.5,\quad \frac{3^3}{6}=4.5,\quad \frac{3^4}{24}=3.375, \qquad\text{sum }=16.375 .$$

    Dividing, and clearing to a common denominator of $131$ (multiply every weight by $8$):

    $$\pi=\left(\tfrac{8}{131},\,\tfrac{24}{131},\,\tfrac{36}{131},\,\tfrac{36}{131},\,\tfrac{27}{131}\right) =(0.061069,\,0.183206,\,0.274809,\,0.274809,\,0.206107).$$

    So $\mathbf{P}(\text{blocked})=\pi_4=27/131=0.206107$: more than one call in five is lost. Two independent checks, both in computes/g5_s3.py section E. (i) Building the discretized $5\times5$ transition matrix with $\delta=10^{-4}$ and solving $\pi=\pi P$ numerically reproduces these five numbers to $9.2\times10^{-16}$ — confirming that $\delta$ really does cancel. (ii) The mean number of busy lines is $\sum_i i\pi_i=2.381679$, which must also equal $a\,(1-\pi_B)=3(1-0.206107)=2.381679$ (offered load minus blocked load); the two agree to $4.4\times10^{-16}$.

    Sizing the exchange. Blocking falls fast in $B$. At $a=3$ erlangs:

    $B$124681012
    $\mathbf{P}(\text{blocked})$0.7500000.5294120.2061070.0521570.0081320.0008100.000055

    Eight lines are the smallest number achieving the industry-standard 1% grade of service ($0.008132<0.01$; seven lines give $0.021864$). Note that $8$ lines serve an offered load of $3$ erlangs — you must over-provision by more than a factor of two, and the factor shrinks as the system gets bigger (this is the economy of scale that makes large exchanges efficient).

    Fig. 3.9 — Erlang-B blocking probability versus the number of lines, for three offered loads (computed from the L18 slide-4 formula). Raising the load from 3 to 8 erlangs (a factor of 2.7) does not raise the lines needed for 1% blocking by the same factor — it takes about $15$ instead of $8$.
    Gotcha. Erlang-B assumes blocked calls vanish — they are lost, not queued and not retried. That is why the chain has no state above $B$ and no waiting room. A system where blocked customers wait in line is a different chain (states $0,1,2,\dots$ unbounded, death rate $B\mu\delta$ once all servers are busy) with a different answer (the Erlang-C formula). Reading the birth and death rates off the physical description, state by state, is the modeling step that does all the work; after that the local-balance machinery is identical.
    Practice 3.10. A small call center receives $\lambda=100$ calls per hour with mean handling time $3$ minutes, and has $B=8$ agents. Calls arriving when all agents are busy are lost. Find the offered load, the blocking probability, and the mean number of busy agents. How many agents are needed to get blocking below 2%?
    Solution

    Convert to consistent units: mean handling time $3$ min $=0.05$ h, so $\mu=1/0.05=20$ calls per hour per agent, and the offered load is $a=\lambda/\mu=100/20=5$ erlangs.

    With $B=8$, the unnormalized weights $a^i/i!$ for $i=0,\dots,8$ are $1,\;5,\;12.5,\;20.8333,\;26.0417,\;26.0417,\;21.7014,\;15.5010,\;9.6881$, summing to $138.30717$. Hence $$\mathbf{P}(\text{blocked})=\pi_8=\frac{9.68812}{138.30717}=0.070048,$$ and $\pi_0=1/138.30717=0.007230$. Mean busy agents $=\sum_i i\pi_i=4.649761$, which also equals $a(1-\pi_B)=5(1-0.070048)=4.649761$ ✓. All values from computes/g5_s3.py section E.

    Scanning upward (same section): $B=9$ gives $0.037$, and $B=10$ gives $0.018385<0.02$. So 10 agents. Adding two agents to a team of eight cuts lost calls from 7% to under 2% — the steep part of the Erlang-B curve is exactly where a couple of extra servers buy the most.

    §3 cheatsheet.
    • Convergence (L17 slide 5, L18 slide 2). Single recurrent class + aperiodic $\Rightarrow r_{ij}(n)\to\pi_j$ for every $i$. Two classes $\Rightarrow$ the limit remembers $X_0$; periodic $\Rightarrow$ no limit (but the $\pi_j$ still are the long-run frequencies).
    • Balance equations. $\displaystyle\pi_j=\sum_k\pi_kp_{kj}$ for each $j$, obtained by letting $n\to\infty$ in $r_{ij}(n)=\sum_kr_{ik}(n-1)p_{kj}$ (finite sums only). They are dependent — they sum to $0=0$ — so drop one and adjoin $\displaystyle\sum_j\pi_j=1$. In matrix form $\pi=\pi P$ with $\pi$ a row vector.
    • Frequency reading (L17 slide 6). $\pi_j$ = long-run fraction of time in $j$; $\pi_jp_{jk}$ = long-run frequency of the transition $j\to k$; balance = flow in equals flow out. Long-run average cost $=\sum_j\pi_jc_j$ (state costs) or $\sum_{j,k}\pi_jp_{jk}d_{jk}$ (transition costs). Also $t_j^*=1/\pi_j$ (§4).
    • Two-state chain (L17 slide 7 / L18 slide 3). $p_{11}=0.5,p_{12}=0.5,p_{21}=0.2,p_{22}=0.8 \Rightarrow \pi=(2/7,\,5/7)$, and both L18 slide-3 answers equal $1/7\approx0.142857$.
    • Birth–death (L17 slide 8). Local balance across each cut: $\pi_ip_i=\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}$, hence $\displaystyle\pi_i=\pi_0\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{p_{k-1}}{q_k}$, with $\pi_0$ from normalization. Valid because the chain is a line — not a general fact.
    • Constant $p,q$. $\rho=p/q$; $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^{\,i}$; $\pi_0=(1-\rho)/(1-\rho^{m+1})$ for finite $m$, $\pi_0=1/(m+1)$ when $\rho=1$. Infinite buffer with $\rho<1$: $\pi_i=(1-\rho)\rho^{\,i}$, $\mathbf{P}(X\ge k)=\rho^k$, $\mathbb{E}[X]=\rho/(1-\rho)$ — which diverges as $\rho\to1$ ($\rho=0.9\Rightarrow9$; $\rho=0.99\Rightarrow99$). No stationary distribution at all if $\rho\ge1$ and the buffer is infinite.
    • Erlang-B (L18 slide 4). Constant birth rate $\lambda\delta$, state-proportional death rate $i\mu\delta$ $\Rightarrow \lambda\pi_{i-1}=i\mu\pi_i \Rightarrow \pi_i=\pi_0a^i/i!$ with $a=\lambda/\mu$ erlangs; blocking $=\pi_B=\dfrac{a^B/B!}{\sum_{i=0}^Ba^i/i!}$ (truncated Poisson). For $a=3$, $B=4$: $\pi_B=27/131=0.2061$; $B=8$ brings it to $0.00813$.

    §4   Absorption probabilities and expected time to absorption

    Sources: L18 slides 5–7 (raster p02) · rec19 Problem 1(a)–(h) · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §7.4 (absorption probability equations; Example 7.11 gambler's ruin; Example 7.12 spider-and-fly, whose chain is B&T Example 7.2 and its Figure 7.19; Example 7.13 and the mean first passage / recurrence time equations; B&T Problem 7.33 for the uniqueness argument, B&T Problem 7.34 for $\pi_s=1/t_s^*$). Every number on this page is recomputed in computes/g5_s4.py (exact rational elimination, an independent numpy.linalg.solve on each system, and Monte-Carlo checks); every figure is generated by computes/g5_s4_figs.py.

    Section 3 answered the long-run question: if a chain has a single aperiodic recurrent class, where is it after a very long time? This section answers the two short-run questions that the long-run answer throws away. Suppose the chain starts in a transient state and there is more than one place it can get stuck. Which trap does it fall into, and how long does the fall take? Steady-state analysis cannot tell you: the steady-state probability of every transient state is $0$ no matter how long the chain lingers there, and if there are two absorbing states the limiting distribution depends on where you started, which is exactly the information the balance equations discard.

    Both questions are answered by the same idea, and the idea is one you already own. In G4 §1 you computed $\mathbb{E}[X]$ by conditioning on a first coin flip; in G2 §3 you got the geometric mean $1/p$ from the recursion $\mathbb{E}[X]=1+(1-p)\mathbb{E}[X]$. Do the same here — condition on the first transition — and the Markov property guarantees that what follows the first transition is a fresh copy of the same problem started at the new state. One unknown per state, one equation per state, and a linear system to solve. The technique is called first-step analysis, and it is the whole of L18 slides 5–7 and the whole of rec19.

    Interpretation. Every formula in this section has the shape "$(\text{answer at }i)=\sum_j p_{ij}\,(\text{answer at }j)\;+\;(\text{cost of the step})$". For a probability the step costs nothing, so $a_i=\sum_j p_{ij}a_j$. For an expected time the step costs one time unit, so $\mu_i=1+\sum_j p_{ij}\mu_j$. If you remember the shape, you can rebuild every equation on this page from scratch, including ones the course never writes down (expected total reward, expected number of visits to a marked state, expected number of ice cream cones — rec19(d)).

    4.1 Absorbing states and absorption probabilities

    Definition (absorbing state; B&T §7.4). A state $k$ is absorbing if $$p_{kk}=1,\qquad p_{kj}=0\ \text{ for all } j\ne k .$$ Once the chain enters $k$ it never leaves, so $\{k\}$ is a recurrent class all by itself. Here $p_{kj}$ is the one-step transition probability $\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=k)$ of §1.

    Why we may always assume the recurrent states are absorbing. The questions of this section are "which recurrent class is entered, and when". The instant the chain enters a recurrent class it can never leave it (§2), so everything that happens afterwards is irrelevant to both questions. We may therefore doctor the chain: take each recurrent class, merge its states into a single new state, and make that state absorbing. This changes nothing about which class is entered or how long that takes, and it leaves us with a chain in which every state is either transient or absorbing (B&T §7.4 does exactly this in its Figure 7.17, where states 4 and 5 are lumped into a single absorbing state 6). All of §4 is written for such a chain.

    Definition (absorption probability; B&T §7.4). Fix one absorbing state $s$. For every state $i$ define $$a_i=\mathbf{P}\big(X_n \text{ eventually equals } s \;\big|\; X_0=i\big),$$ the probability that the chain is ultimately absorbed at $s$ given that it starts at $i$. The subscript $i$ is the starting state; $s$ is fixed once and for all before you write a single equation.
    Fig. 4.1 — First-step analysis, the picture behind every equation in this section. Condition on the first transition; by the Markov property the rest of the trip is the same problem started at the new state. The only difference between the two equations is whether the step itself is charged (L18 slides 5–6, B&T §7.4).
    Theorem (absorption probability equations; L18 slide 5, B&T §7.4). In a chain in which every state is transient or absorbing, and with $s$ a fixed absorbing state, the numbers $a_1,\ldots,a_m$ are the unique solution of $$a_s=1,\qquad a_i=0 \ \text{ for every absorbing } i\ne s,\qquad a_i=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ij}\,a_j\ \text{ for every transient } i .$$

    Derivation, every step. The two boundary conditions are immediate from the definition: if $X_0=s$ the chain is already absorbed at $s$, so the event has probability $1$; if $X_0=i$ is some other absorbing state, the chain never moves, so it never reaches $s$ and the probability is $0$. For a transient $i$, let $A$ be the event "$X_n$ eventually equals $s$". Condition on the first transition: $$a_i=\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_0=i) =\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_0=i,\,X_1=j)\,\mathbf{P}(X_1=j\mid X_0=i),$$ which is the total probability theorem (G1 §2) applied to the partition of the sample space by the value of $X_1$, everything conditioned on $X_0=i$. The second factor is by definition $p_{ij}$. For the first factor, note that $A$ is an event about $X_1,X_2,\ldots$; by the Markov property, once we know $X_1=j$, the extra conditioning on $X_0=i$ tells us nothing more about the future, so $$\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_0=i,\,X_1=j)=\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_1=j)=a_j ,$$ the last equality because the chain is time-homogeneous — the probability of eventually hitting $s$ starting from $j$ at time $1$ is the same as starting from $j$ at time $0$. Substituting both factors, $$a_i=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ij}\,a_j .\qquad\blacksquare$$

    Why the solution is unique (the gap L18 slide 5 leaves open). The slide asserts "unique solution" and stops. Here is the argument (B&T Problem 7.33). Order the states so that the transient ones come first, and let $Q$ be the square block of $p_{ij}$ with both $i$ and $j$ transient, $b$ the column vector of $\sum_{j\text{ absorbing}}p_{ij}a_j$ (a known number for each transient $i$, since $a_j$ is $1$ at $s$ and $0$ elsewhere). The transient equations read $x=Qx+b$, i.e. $(I-Q)x=b$. Now $(Q^n)_{ik}=\mathbf{P}(X_n=k,\ X_1,\ldots,X_{n-1}\text{ all transient} \mid X_0=i)$, so the row sums of $Q^n$ equal $\mathbf{P}(\text{not yet absorbed by time } n\mid X_0=i)$, which tends to $0$ because absorption happens with probability $1$ from any transient state (§2). Hence $Q^n\to0$, the series $\sum_{n\ge0}Q^n$ converges, and $(I-Q)\sum_{n\ge0}Q^n=I$; so $I-Q$ is invertible and $x=(I-Q)^{-1}b$ is the one and only solution. The identical argument covers the $\mu_i$ and $t_i$ systems below.
    Gotcha. $a_i$ is defined relative to one particular target $s$. Change the target and every number changes. If a chain has absorbing states $4$ and $5$, the vector $(a_1,a_2,a_3)$ for target $4$ and the vector for target $5$ are different solutions of different systems — though of course they must add up to $1$ componentwise, because absorption somewhere is certain. Use that as a free check on your arithmetic.
    Example 4.1 (L18 slide 5 — the slide poses the question and leaves every $a_i$ blank; solved here in full). The chain has five states. States $4$ and $5$ are absorbing. The transient rows are $$p_{14}=0.2,\quad p_{12}=0.5,\quad p_{13}=0.3;\qquad p_{21}=0.4,\quad p_{23}=0.6;\qquad p_{32}=0.8,\quad p_{35}=0.2 ,$$ each summing to $1$ as it must. Find $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }4\mid X_0=i)$ for every $i$.
    Fig. 4.2 — The chain of L18 slide 5 (the handout prints the circles empty; the numbering $4$, $5$ for the two absorbing states is the one the slide's text uses). Blue = transient, orange = absorbing. The computed $a_i$ are printed beside each transient state.

    Step 1 — boundary conditions. The target is $s=4$, so $a_4=1$; the other absorbing state gives $a_5=0$. These are the two blanks the slide leaves for lecture.

    Step 2 — one equation per transient state. Apply $a_i=\sum_j p_{ij}a_j$ to $i=1,2,3$, substituting the known boundary values as we go: $$a_1=0.2\,a_4+0.5\,a_2+0.3\,a_3=0.2+0.5a_2+0.3a_3,$$ $$a_2=0.4\,a_1+0.6\,a_3,$$ $$a_3=0.8\,a_2+0.2\,a_5=0.8a_2 .$$

    Step 3 — eliminate. The third equation already gives $a_3$ in terms of $a_2$. Put it into the second: $$a_2=0.4a_1+0.6(0.8a_2)=0.4a_1+0.48a_2 \;\Longrightarrow\; (1-0.48)a_2=0.4a_1 \;\Longrightarrow\; 0.52\,a_2=0.4\,a_1 ,$$ so $a_2=\frac{0.4}{0.52}a_1=\frac{10}{13}a_1$ and therefore $a_3=0.8\cdot\frac{10}{13}a_1=\frac{8}{13}a_1$.

    Step 4 — close the loop on $a_1$. Substitute both into the first equation: $$a_1=0.2+0.5\cdot\tfrac{10}{13}a_1+0.3\cdot\tfrac{8}{13}a_1 =0.2+\frac{5+2.4}{13}a_1=0.2+\frac{7.4}{13}a_1 .$$ Move the $a_1$ term across: $\left(1-\frac{7.4}{13}\right)a_1=0.2$, i.e. $\frac{5.6}{13}a_1=0.2$, hence $$a_1=\frac{0.2\cdot13}{5.6}=\frac{2.6}{5.6}=\frac{13}{28}=0.464286 .$$

    Step 5 — back-substitute. $$a_2=\frac{10}{13}\cdot\frac{13}{28}=\frac{10}{28}=\frac{5}{14}=0.357143,\qquad a_3=\frac{8}{13}\cdot\frac{13}{28}=\frac{8}{28}=\frac{2}{7}=0.285714 .$$ (Keys L18_a1, L18_a2, L18_a3; a $400{,}000$-run simulation started at state $1$ lands in state $4$ a fraction $0.463990$ of the time, L18_a1_mc.)

    Sanity checks. All three are between $0$ and $1$, as probabilities must be. They are ordered $a_1>a_2>a_3$, which is right: state $1$ is the only state with a direct arc into $4$, and state $3$ is the only state with a direct arc into the rival absorbing state $5$. And the absorption probabilities for the target $5$ are the complements $15/28$, $9/14$, $5/7$.

    Interpretation. Notice what the answer is not. It is not "the fraction of arcs pointing toward $4$", and it is not determined by the one-step probabilities of state $i$ alone: $a_2=5/14$ even though state $2$ has no arc to either absorbing state. The system of equations is doing the work of summing over infinitely many paths, each weighted by its probability. Solving three linear equations replaces an infinite sum — that is the entire payoff of first-step analysis.
    Practice 4.1. In the chain of Fig. 4.2, what is the probability that the chain started at state $2$ visits state $1$ at least once before being absorbed? (Hint: this is an absorption question for a modified chain.)
    Solution

    Make state $1$ absorbing (we only care about reaching it, not what happens after) and keep $5$ absorbing; state $4$ is unreachable without passing through $1$, so it can be dropped. Let $h_i$ be the probability of reaching $1$ before $5$. Then $h_1=1$, $h_5=0$, and $$h_2=0.4h_1+0.6h_3=0.4+0.6h_3,\qquad h_3=0.8h_2+0.2h_5=0.8h_2 .$$ Substituting, $h_2=0.4+0.6(0.8h_2)=0.4+0.48h_2$, so $0.52h_2=0.4$ and $h_2=0.4/0.52=10/13=0.769231$. Note this is exactly the ratio $a_2/a_1$ found in Step 3 above — and that is no accident: to be absorbed at $4$ starting from $2$ you must pass through $1$ (it is the only state with an arc to $4$), so $a_2=h_2\,a_1$ by the multiplication rule plus the Markov property.

    Practice 4.2. A chain has transient states $1,2$ and absorbing states $A,B$ with $p_{12}=1/2$, $p_{1A}=1/2$, $p_{21}=1/4$, $p_{2B}=3/4$. Find $\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }A)$ from each transient state.
    Solution

    Take $s=A$, so $a_A=1$, $a_B=0$, and $$a_1=\tfrac12a_2+\tfrac12(1),\qquad a_2=\tfrac14a_1+\tfrac34(0)=\tfrac14a_1 .$$ Substitute the second into the first: $a_1=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac14a_1+\tfrac12=\tfrac18a_1+\tfrac12$, so $\tfrac78a_1=\tfrac12$ and $a_1=\tfrac{4}{7}=0.571429$. Then $a_2=\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac47=\tfrac17=0.142857$ (keys prac_2_a1, prac_2_a2). Check: from state $2$ you are absorbed at $A$ only by first returning to $1$ (probability $1/4$) and then being absorbed at $A$ from there, so $a_2=\tfrac14a_1$ — consistent.

    4.2 Expected time to absorption

    Definition (expected time to absorption; L18 slide 6, B&T §7.4). $$\mu_i=\mathbb{E}\big[\text{number of transitions until absorption}\mid X_0=i\big] =\mathbb{E}\big[\min\{n\ge0:X_n\text{ is recurrent}\}\mid X_0=i\big].$$ Note $\mu_i=0$ whenever $i$ is itself absorbing (the minimum is achieved at $n=0$), and $\mu_i$ does not care which absorbing state is reached — only when.
    Theorem (expected-time equations; L18 slide 6). The numbers $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_m$ are the unique solution of $$\mu_i=0\ \text{ for every absorbing } i,\qquad \mu_i=1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ij}\,\mu_j\ \text{ for every transient } i .$$

    Derivation, every step (L18 slide 6 states the recursion but derives nothing). Let $T$ be the number of transitions until absorption. Starting from a transient $i$ the chain certainly makes at least one transition, so write $T=1+T'$, where $T'$ is the number of further transitions needed after the first one. Apply the total expectation theorem (G4 §1), conditioning on $X_1$: $$\mu_i=\mathbb{E}[T\mid X_0=i]=1+\mathbb{E}[T'\mid X_0=i] =1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{P}(X_1=j\mid X_0=i)\;\mathbb{E}[T'\mid X_0=i,\,X_1=j].$$ The first equality splits off the deterministic $1$ using linearity of expectation. In the last term, $T'$ counts transitions of the chain from time $1$ onward; by the Markov property and time homogeneity, given $X_1=j$ that count has exactly the distribution of $T$ for a chain started at $j$, and the extra conditioning on $X_0=i$ is irrelevant. Hence $\mathbb{E}[T'\mid X_0=i,X_1=j]=\mu_j$, and with $\mathbf{P}(X_1=j\mid X_0=i)=p_{ij}$, $$\mu_i=1+\sum_{j=1}^m p_{ij}\,\mu_j .\qquad\blacksquare$$ Uniqueness follows from the same $Q^n\to0$ argument as in §4.1: the system is $(I-Q)\mu=\mathbf{1}$.

    Gotcha — the two most common slips. (i) Forgetting the leading $1$. Without it the system reads $\mu=Q\mu$, whose unique solution is $\mu=0$: the chain would be absorbed instantly. The $1$ is the cost of the step you just took. (ii) Writing $\mu_i=1+\sum_j p_{ij}\mu_j$ for an absorbing $i$ too. That is wrong — an absorbing $i$ has $\mu_i=0$, not $1$. Equivalently: the sum on the right runs over all $j$, but the terms with $j$ absorbing contribute $p_{ij}\cdot0=0$, so in practice you only ever carry the transient terms.
    Example 4.2 (L18 slide 6 — again the slide asks and leaves the answer blank). The slide modifies the chain of Fig. 4.2: the absorbing state $4$ is deleted and the probability $0.2$ that led there is added to the arc $1\to3$, giving $p_{13}=0.3+0.2=0.5$. Now $5$ is the only absorbing state: $$p_{12}=0.5,\ p_{13}=0.5;\qquad p_{21}=0.4,\ p_{23}=0.6;\qquad p_{32}=0.8,\ p_{35}=0.2 .$$ Find the expected number of transitions to absorption from each state.
    Fig. 4.3 — The chain of L18 slide 6, with the computed $\mu_i$ printed beside each state. Because there is only one absorbing state, "absorption probability" is trivially $1$ and only the timing question is interesting.

    Step 1 — boundary condition. $\mu_5=0$ (the blank on the slide).

    Step 2 — one equation per transient state. $$\mu_1=1+0.5\mu_2+0.5\mu_3,$$ $$\mu_2=1+0.4\mu_1+0.6\mu_3,$$ $$\mu_3=1+0.8\mu_2+0.2\mu_5=1+0.8\mu_2 .$$

    Step 3 — eliminate $\mu_3$. Substitute $\mu_3=1+0.8\mu_2$ into the second equation: $$\mu_2=1+0.4\mu_1+0.6(1+0.8\mu_2)=1.6+0.4\mu_1+0.48\mu_2 \;\Longrightarrow\;0.52\,\mu_2=1.6+0.4\,\mu_1 .$$

    Step 4 — eliminate $\mu_2$ and $\mu_3$ from the first equation. $$\mu_1=1+0.5\mu_2+0.5(1+0.8\mu_2)=1.5+0.5\mu_2+0.4\mu_2=1.5+0.9\,\mu_2 .$$ Now use $\mu_2=(1.6+0.4\mu_1)/0.52$: $$\mu_1=1.5+0.9\cdot\frac{1.6+0.4\mu_1}{0.52}.$$ Multiply both sides by $0.52$: $$0.52\mu_1=0.78+0.9(1.6+0.4\mu_1)=0.78+1.44+0.36\mu_1 ,$$ so $(0.52-0.36)\mu_1=2.22$, i.e. $0.16\,\mu_1=2.22$ and $$\mu_1=\frac{2.22}{0.16}=13.875=\frac{111}{8}.$$

    Step 5 — back-substitute. $$\mu_2=\frac{1.6+0.4(13.875)}{0.52}=\frac{1.6+5.55}{0.52}=\frac{7.15}{0.52}=13.75=\frac{55}{4}, \qquad \mu_3=1+0.8(13.75)=1+11=12 .$$ (Keys L18_mu1, L18_mu2, L18_mu3; simulation from state $1$ gives a mean of $13.828920$ transitions, L18_mu1_mc.)

    Why so long? Only one arc leaves the transient triangle, the $0.2$ from state $3$ to state $5$, and the chain reaches state $3$ only intermittently. A rough sanity estimate: the chain spends roughly a third of its transient time at state $3$ and escapes with probability $0.2$ per visit, so absorption should take on the order of $3/0.2\approx15$ steps — the exact $13.875$ is in the right neighborhood.

    Recipe — choosing the right linear system.
    1. Classify first. Identify the recurrent classes and the transient states (§2). Lump every recurrent class into one absorbing state unless the question asks something about behavior inside a class.
    2. Decide what the unknown is. "Which trap?" $\Rightarrow$ absorption probabilities $a_i$, one target at a time. "How long until any trap?" $\Rightarrow$ expected times $\mu_i$. "How long until one specific state $s$?" $\Rightarrow$ mean first passage times $t_i$ (§4.4): make $s$ absorbing and re-use the $\mu$ machinery.
    3. Write the boundary conditions first. $a_s=1$ and $a_i=0$ at the other absorbing states; or $\mu_i=0$ at every absorbing state; or $t_s=0$.
    4. Write one equation per transient state by first-step analysis, substituting the boundary values immediately. Charge $+1$ if and only if you are counting time.
    5. Solve and check. Probabilities must lie in $[0,1]$ and the $a_i$ over all absorbing targets must sum to $1$; expected times must be positive and at least $1$ from any transient state.
    Fig. 4.4 — Which system do I write? The three top branches are L18 slides 5, 6 and 7; the two bottom refinements are rec19(e) (conditioning on the absorbing state, §4.5) and rec19(h) (mean recurrence time, §4.4).
    Example 4.3 (spider and fly — B&T Example 7.12, on the chain of B&T Example 7.2 and its Figure 7.19, with $m=4$). A fly moves along a line in unit increments: at each time period it moves one unit left with probability $0.3$, one unit right with probability $0.3$, and stays put with probability $0.4$, independently of everything else. Spiders sit at positions $1$ and $m$; if the fly lands on a spider it is caught and the process ends. Take $m=4$, so the states are the fly's positions $1,2,3,4$ with $1$ and $4$ absorbing and $$p_{21}=p_{23}=0.3,\ p_{22}=0.4;\qquad p_{32}=p_{34}=0.3,\ p_{33}=0.4 .$$ Find (i) the expected time until the fly is caught, and (ii) the probability it is caught by the right-hand spider.
    Fig. 4.5 — The spider-and-fly chain for $m=4$ (B&T Example 7.2, its Figure 7.19). Self-loops of probability $0.4$ are the fly standing still.

    (i) Expected time. Boundary: $\mu_1=\mu_4=0$. For the two transient states, $$\mu_2=1+0.3\mu_1+0.4\mu_2+0.3\mu_3=1+0.4\mu_2+0.3\mu_3,$$ $$\mu_3=1+0.3\mu_2+0.4\mu_3+0.3\mu_4=1+0.3\mu_2+0.4\mu_3 .$$ The chain is symmetric under $2\leftrightarrow3$ (relabel positions by reflection and the transition probabilities are unchanged), so $\mu_2=\mu_3=:\mu$. Both equations then collapse to the same one: $$\mu=1+0.4\mu+0.3\mu=1+0.7\mu\;\Longrightarrow\;0.3\,\mu=1\;\Longrightarrow\;\mu=\frac{10}{3}=3.333333 .$$ (Keys spider_mu2, spider_mu3; simulation gives $3.331443$.) If you do not want to invoke symmetry, eliminate directly. The second equation rearranges to $0.6\mu_3=1+0.3\mu_2$, i.e. $\mu_3=(1+0.3\mu_2)/0.6$. The first rearranges to $0.6\mu_2=1+0.3\mu_3$, and substituting, $$0.3\mu_3=0.3\cdot\frac{1+0.3\mu_2}{0.6}=0.5\left(1+0.3\mu_2\right)=0.5+0.15\mu_2 ,$$ so $0.6\mu_2=1+0.5+0.15\mu_2$, giving $0.45\mu_2=1.5$ and $\mu_2=10/3$ as before.

    Interpretation of $10/3$. From either interior state the fly escapes the pair $\{2,3\}$ only by the $0.3$ arc into a spider; the self-loops and the $2\leftrightarrow3$ moves waste time. Because the two interior states are interchangeable, the effective escape probability per step is exactly $0.3$, and the time to absorption is geometric with parameter $0.3$ — mean $1/0.3=10/3$ (G2 §3). The linear system just rediscovered the geometric mean.

    (ii) Which spider. Take $s=4$, so $a_4=1$, $a_1=0$, and $$a_2=0.3(0)+0.4a_2+0.3a_3\;\Longrightarrow\;0.6a_2=0.3a_3 ,$$ $$a_3=0.3a_2+0.4a_3+0.3(1)\;\Longrightarrow\;0.6a_3=0.3a_2+0.3 .$$ From the first, $a_3=2a_2$. Substituting into the second, $0.6(2a_2)=0.3a_2+0.3$, i.e. $1.2a_2-0.3a_2=0.3$, so $0.9a_2=0.3$ and $$a_2=\frac{1}{3}=0.333333,\qquad a_3=\frac{2}{3}=0.666667 .$$ (Keys spider_a2, spider_a3; simulation from state $2$ gives $0.332742$.) These are the linear interpolation $a_i=(i-1)/(m-1)$ — the signature of a symmetric random walk, and exactly the fair-game gambler's ruin formula of §4.3. The self-loop probability $0.4$ cancels out of the absorption probabilities entirely: standing still cannot change where you eventually end up, only how long you take to get there.

    Practice 4.3. For the chain of Practice 4.2 ($p_{12}=1/2$, $p_{1A}=1/2$, $p_{21}=1/4$, $p_{2B}=3/4$), find the expected number of transitions to absorption from each transient state.
    Solution

    $\mu_A=\mu_B=0$, and $$\mu_1=1+\tfrac12\mu_2,\qquad \mu_2=1+\tfrac14\mu_1 .$$ Substitute the second into the first: $\mu_1=1+\tfrac12\left(1+\tfrac14\mu_1\right)=\tfrac32+\tfrac18\mu_1$, so $\tfrac78\mu_1=\tfrac32$ and $\mu_1=\tfrac{12}{7}=1.714286$ (key prac_2_mu1). Then $\mu_2=1+\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac{12}{7}=1+\tfrac37=\tfrac{10}{7}=1.428571$ (prac_2_mu2). Both exceed $1$, as they must.

    Practice 4.4. Redo the spider-and-fly expected time for $m=5$ (transient states $2,3,4$, same $0.3/0.4/0.3$ rule). Is $\mu_3$ bigger or smaller than $\mu_2$, and why?
    Solution

    $\mu_1=\mu_5=0$ and $$\mu_2=1+0.4\mu_2+0.3\mu_3,\qquad \mu_3=1+0.3\mu_2+0.4\mu_3+0.3\mu_4,\qquad \mu_4=1+0.3\mu_3+0.4\mu_4 .$$ Symmetry gives $\mu_2=\mu_4$. The first equation becomes $0.6\mu_2=1+0.3\mu_3$, so $\mu_2=(1+0.3\mu_3)/0.6$. The middle equation becomes $0.6\mu_3=1+0.6\mu_2=1+(1+0.3\mu_3)$, i.e. $0.6\mu_3-0.3\mu_3=2$, so $0.3\mu_3=2$ and $\mu_3=\tfrac{20}{3}=6.666667$. Then $\mu_2=\mu_4=(1+0.3\cdot\tfrac{20}{3})/0.6=(1+2)/0.6=5$ (keys prac_4_mu2, prac_4_mu3, prac_4_mu4). $\mu_3>\mu_2$ because the middle state is two steps from either spider while the outer transient states are one step from one of them.

    4.3 Gambler's ruin

    The single most important absorption problem in the course, because it is the one with a closed form. B&T give it as Example 7.11.

    Example 4.4 (gambler's ruin; B&T Example 7.11 and its Figure 7.18). A gambler bets \$1 on each of a sequence of independent rounds, winning with probability $p$ and losing with probability $1-p$. She plays until she either accumulates a target fortune of \$$m$ or runs out of money. Starting with \$$i$, what is the probability $a_i$ that she reaches \$$m$, and what is the expected number $D_i$ of rounds played?
    Fig. 4.6 — The gambler's ruin chain (B&T Example 7.11, its Figure 7.18). The state is the current fortune; $0$ (ruin) and $m$ (target) are absorbing.

    Setting up. The state $X_n$ is the fortune after $n$ rounds, taking values $0,1,\ldots,m$. States $0$ and $m$ are absorbing; every other state has $p_{i,i+1}=p$ and $p_{i,i-1}=1-p$. Fix the target $s=m$, so $a_i$ is the probability of winning. First-step analysis gives $$a_0=0,\qquad a_m=1,\qquad a_i=p\,a_{i+1}+(1-p)\,a_{i-1},\quad i=1,\ldots,m-1 .$$

    The trick that makes it solvable. Because $p+(1-p)=1$ we may write the left-hand side as $a_i=p\,a_i+(1-p)\,a_i$ — this is the only clever step in the whole derivation. Subtracting, $$p\,a_i+(1-p)a_i=p\,a_{i+1}+(1-p)a_{i-1} \;\Longrightarrow\; p\,(a_{i+1}-a_i)=(1-p)\,(a_i-a_{i-1}).$$ Introduce the successive differences $$\delta_i=a_{i+1}-a_i,\qquad i=0,1,\ldots,m-1,$$ and the odds ratio $$\rho=\frac{1-p}{p},$$ the ratio of the probability of losing a round to the probability of winning one. The relation above becomes simply $\delta_i=\rho\,\delta_{i-1}$ for $i=1,\ldots,m-1$, so by induction $$\delta_i=\rho^{\,i}\delta_0,\qquad i=0,\ldots,m-1 .$$

    Pin down $\delta_0$. The differences telescope: $$\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\delta_i=(a_1-a_0)+(a_2-a_1)+\cdots+(a_m-a_{m-1})=a_m-a_0=1-0=1 .$$ Substituting $\delta_i=\rho^i\delta_0$, $$\delta_0\left(1+\rho+\rho^2+\cdots+\rho^{\,m-1}\right)=1 \;\Longrightarrow\; \delta_0=\frac{1}{1+\rho+\cdots+\rho^{\,m-1}} .$$

    Assemble $a_i$. Since $a_0=0$, $$a_i=a_0+\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\delta_k=\delta_0\left(1+\rho+\cdots+\rho^{\,i-1}\right) =\frac{1+\rho+\cdots+\rho^{\,i-1}}{1+\rho+\cdots+\rho^{\,m-1}} .$$ Now evaluate the two finite geometric sums (G2 §3). If $\rho\ne1$, i.e. $p\ne\tfrac12$, the numerator is $(1-\rho^{\,i})/(1-\rho)$ and the denominator is $(1-\rho^{\,m})/(1-\rho)$, so the common factor $1/(1-\rho)$ cancels; if $\rho=1$ each sum is just its number of terms. Therefore $$\boxed{\;a_i=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1-\rho^{\,i}}{1-\rho^{\,m}}, & p\ne\tfrac12,\\[2ex] \dfrac{i}{m}, & p=\tfrac12,\end{cases}\qquad \rho=\frac{1-p}{p}. \;}$$

    Expected duration. The same chain, now counting time: $D_0=D_m=0$ and $$D_i=1+p\,D_{i+1}+(1-p)\,D_{i-1},\qquad i=1,\ldots,m-1 .$$ This is the $a$-recursion with an extra constant $1$, so its solution is any particular solution plus the general solution of the homogeneous problem. For a particular solution try $D_i=ci$ (linear in $i$): $$ci=1+p\,c(i+1)+(1-p)\,c(i-1)=1+ci+c\big(p-(1-p)\big)=1+ci+c(2p-1),$$ so $0=1+c(2p-1)$ and $c=1/(1-2p)$, valid when $p\ne\tfrac12$. The homogeneous equation $h_i=p\,h_{i+1}+(1-p)h_{i-1}$ is the one we just solved: its solutions are $h_i=A+B\rho^{\,i}$. Hence $D_i=\dfrac{i}{1-2p}+A+B\rho^{\,i}$. Impose $D_0=0$: $A+B=0$, so $B=-A$ and $D_i=\dfrac{i}{1-2p}+A\left(1-\rho^{\,i}\right)$. Impose $D_m=0$: $$\frac{m}{1-2p}+A\left(1-\rho^{\,m}\right)=0\;\Longrightarrow\;A=-\frac{m}{(1-2p)\left(1-\rho^{\,m}\right)} .$$ Substituting and recognizing $a_i=(1-\rho^i)/(1-\rho^m)$, $$\boxed{\;D_i=\begin{cases}\dfrac{i-m\,a_i}{1-2p}, & p\ne\tfrac12,\\[2ex] i\,(m-i), & p=\tfrac12.\end{cases}\;}$$ For $p=\tfrac12$ the linear trial fails (the coefficient $1-2p$ vanishes) and one tries a quadratic: $D_i=i(m-i)$ works, since $1+\tfrac12\big[(i+1)(m-i-1)+(i-1)(m-i+1)\big]=1+\tfrac12\big[2im-2i^2-2\big]=i(m-i)$. Every one of these closed forms is checked in computes/g5_s4.py against a direct numpy.linalg.solve of the $(m-1)\times(m-1)$ system, for $(p,m)\in\{(0.5,10),(0.4,10),(0.6,10),(0.45,20)\}$: agreement to $3\times10^{-13}$ or better.

    Fig. 4.7 — Gambler's ruin with target $m=20$. Left: $a_i$ against the starting fortune. The fair game $p=0.5$ is the straight line $i/m$; a $5$-point swing in $p$ bends it into an exponential. Right: expected duration $D_i$, maximized in the middle and — counter-intuitively — largest for the fair game. Values from the closed forms above (computes/g5_s4.py).
    Interpretation — why a $5\%$ edge is fatal. With $m=20$ and a starting stake of $i=10$ the probability of doubling up before going broke is $0.500000$ at $p=0.50$ but only $0.118501$ at $p=0.45$ and $0.017046$ at $p=0.40$ (keys ruin_tab_p50_m20_i10, ruin_tab_p45_m20_i10, ruin_tab_p40_m20_i10). The reason is the $\rho^{\,i}$: a small edge per round compounds geometrically over the many rounds the game lasts. Take the limit $m\to\infty$ (playing forever against an infinitely rich house). If $p<\tfrac12$ then $\rho>1$, so $\rho^m\to\infty$ and $a_i=(1-\rho^i)/(1-\rho^m)\to0$ for every fixed $i$: under unfavorable odds you are ruined with probability $1$ no matter how large your bankroll. If $p>\tfrac12$ then $\rho<1$ and $a_i\to1-\rho^{\,i}$, a number strictly less than $1$: even with the edge, ruin has positive probability $\rho^{\,i}$ — at $p=0.55$ and $i=10$ the chance of eventually reaching any target, however large, is $1-(9/11)^{10}=0.865569$ (key ruin_p55_inf_i10).
    Gotcha — the duration is longest when the game is fair. The right panel of Fig. 4.7 shows $D_{10}=100$ rounds at $p=0.5$ but only $76.299894$ at $p=0.45$ (keys ruin_tab_p50_m20_i10_D, ruin_tab_p45_m20_i10_D; simulation gives $76.163450$). Drift in either direction shortens the game, because drift pushes you to a boundary; a fair game diffuses aimlessly. So "the game ended quickly" is no evidence that it was rigged in your favor. A second surprise: at $p=0.45$, $m=100$, $i=50$ the win probability is $4.4\times10^{-5}$ yet the expected duration is $499.956099$ rounds (keys ruin_p45_m100_i50_a, ruin_p45_m100_i50_D) — you lose almost surely, but slowly, which is precisely what makes casinos profitable and gamblers hopeful.
    Practice 4.5. You sit down at a table with \$50 and decide to play \$1 hands until you either double your money or go broke. Each hand wins with probability $p=0.48$. What are your chances, and how long will you be there?
    Solution

    Here $m=100$, $i=50$, $\rho=(1-0.48)/0.48=0.52/0.48=13/12\approx1.083333$. Then $$a_{50}=\frac{1-\rho^{50}}{1-\rho^{100}}=0.017949$$ (key prac_3_a) — under $2\%$, against $50\%$ for a fair game (prac_3_a_fair). The expected number of hands is $$D_{50}=\frac{50-100\,a_{50}}{1-2(0.48)}=\frac{50-1.7949}{0.04}=1205.128670$$ (key prac_3_D), versus $50\cdot50=2500$ for a fair game (prac_3_D_fair). So: about twelve hundred hands, and you almost certainly leave with nothing. The $2\%$ house edge per hand becomes a $98\%$ house edge over the session.

    Practice 4.6. Show that in a fair game ($p=1/2$) the expected duration $D_i=i(m-i)$ can be read off from a single conservation statement, without solving any recursion.
    Solution

    In a fair game the fortune is a martingale: $\mathbb{E}[X_{n+1}\mid X_n]=X_n$, so $\mathbb{E}[X_n]=i$ for all $n$, and at absorption $\mathbb{E}[X_\infty]=m\,a_i+0\cdot(1-a_i)=i$, giving $a_i=i/m$ — the fair-game absorption formula with no algebra. For the duration, apply the same idea to $X_n^2-n$: since each step changes $X$ by $\pm1$ with equal probability, $\mathbb{E}[X_{n+1}^2\mid X_n]=\tfrac12(X_n+1)^2+\tfrac12(X_n-1)^2=X_n^2+1$, so $\mathbb{E}[X_n^2]-n$ is constant, equal to $i^2$. At absorption $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=m^2a_i=m^2(i/m)=mi$, so $mi-D_i=i^2$ and $D_i=mi-i^2=i(m-i)$. (This martingale argument is not required by 6.041 — it is offered as a cross-check on the algebra above, and it reproduces $D_{10}=10\cdot10=100$ for $m=20$.)

    4.4 Mean first passage and mean recurrence times

    L18 slide 7 takes the $\mu$ machinery and points it at a different question: not "when do I get stuck" but "when do I first reach a particular state $s$", in a chain that need not have any absorbing states at all.

    Definition (L18 slide 7, B&T §7.4). Consider a chain with a single recurrent class and fix a recurrent state $s$.
    • The mean first passage time from $i$ to $s$ is $$t_i=\mathbb{E}\big[\min\{n\ge0:X_n=s\}\mid X_0=i\big].$$ Note the $n\ge0$: starting at $s$ costs nothing, so $t_s=0$.
    • The mean recurrence time of $s$ is $$t_s^{*}=\mathbb{E}\big[\min\{n\ge1:X_n=s\}\mid X_0=s\big],$$ the expected number of steps to return to $s$. The $n\ge1$ is what makes it interesting.
    Theorem (L18 slide 7). The $t_i$ are the unique solution of $$t_s=0,\qquad t_i=1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{ij}\,t_j\ \ \text{for all } i\ne s ,$$ and then $$t_s^{*}=1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{sj}\,t_j .$$

    Derivation. For the $t_i$: the transitions out of $s$ are irrelevant to how long it takes to first reach $s$, so replace the chain by one in which $s$ has been made absorbing ($p_{ss}=1$, $p_{sj}=0$ for $j\ne s$). In the new chain every other state is transient (they were all in one recurrent class, and now every path is eventually swallowed by $s$), and "time to reach $s$" is literally "time to absorption". So the equations are the $\mu$ equations of §4.2, verbatim, with $t_s=0$ playing the role of $\mu_{\text{absorbing}}=0$. Uniqueness is the same $Q^n\to0$ argument.

    Derivation of the recurrence formula (L18 slide 7 states it without proof). Starting at $s$, the chain must make at least one transition — that is what $n\ge1$ means — landing at $j$ with probability $p_{sj}$. From $j$ the remaining number of steps to reach $s$ has mean $t_j$ (this is the first passage quantity, and it is legitimate to use it even for $j=s$, where $t_s=0$ correctly records that no further steps are needed). By the total expectation theorem, conditioning on $X_1$, $$t_s^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^m p_{sj}\big(1+t_j\big)=\sum_j p_{sj}+\sum_j p_{sj}t_j=1+\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{sj}\,t_j ,$$ using $\sum_j p_{sj}=1$. $\blacksquare$

    Property (the bridge back to §3; B&T Problem 7.34). For a chain with a single recurrent aperiodic class, $$\pi_s=\frac{1}{t_s^{*}} .$$ Why. By §3, $\pi_s$ is the long-run fraction of time spent at $s$. Over $n$ steps the chain visits $s$ about $n\pi_s$ times, and consecutive visits are separated by an average of $t_s^{*}$ steps, so $n\approx (n\pi_s)\,t_s^{*}$, giving $\pi_s t_s^{*}=1$. (A frequency and its mean gap are always reciprocal — the same statement as "if buses come at rate $\lambda$, the mean headway is $1/\lambda$", G4 §3.)
    Interpretation — $\pi_s=1/t_s^{*}$ is the most reusable sentence in this note. Read it as a statement about frequency versus gap, not as a formula to memorize. $\pi_s$ answers "what fraction of my life do I spend at $s$?" and $t_s^{*}$ answers "how long between consecutive visits to $s$?" — and those two questions are the same question asked in opposite units, exactly like "3 buses per hour" and "20 minutes apart". Three consequences worth carrying around: (i) a state you visit one week in four has a mean return time of four weeks, no matter how complicated the rest of the chain is; (ii) a rare state ($\pi_s$ small) is automatically a long-wait state ($t_s^{*}$ large), which is why "it hardly ever happens" and "when it does, you waited ages" are not two independent facts about a chain; (iii) it gives you a free cross-check whenever you have computed a steady state one way and a first-passage time another — as Example 4.5 does three separate times. It also gives a cheap route to a mean recurrence time you would otherwise get by solving a linear system: find $\pi$ from the balance equations of §3 and invert it.
    Example 4.5 (B&T Example 7.13, the up-to-date/behind chain of B&T Example 7.1). States $1$ = up-to-date, $2$ = behind, with $p_{11}=0.8$, $p_{12}=0.2$, $p_{21}=0.6$, $p_{22}=0.4$. Find the mean first passage time to state $1$ from state $2$, the mean recurrence time of state $1$, and check it against the steady-state probability.

    First passage. $t_1=0$ and $$t_2=1+p_{21}t_1+p_{22}t_2=1+0.6(0)+0.4t_2=1+0.4t_2 ,$$ so $0.6t_2=1$ and $t_2=\dfrac{1}{0.6}=\dfrac53=1.666667$ (key bt713_t2). Interpretation: each week you are behind, you have probability $0.6$ of catching up, so the wait is geometric with parameter $0.6$ and mean $1/0.6$.

    Recurrence. $$t_1^{*}=1+p_{11}t_1+p_{12}t_2=1+0.8(0)+0.2\cdot\frac53=1+\frac13=\frac43=1.333333$$ (key bt713_t1star).

    Cross-check with §3. $\pi_1=1/t_1^{*}=3/4=0.75$ and $\pi_2=1/4$. Solving the balance equations directly, $\pi_1=0.8\pi_1+0.6\pi_2$ with $\pi_1+\pi_2=1$ gives $0.2\pi_1=0.6(1-\pi_1)$, so $0.8\pi_1=0.6$ and $\pi_1=0.75$ — and raising the transition matrix to the $60$th power gives $(P^{60})_{11}=0.750000$ (key bt713_pi_matrixpower). Three independent routes, one answer.

    Practice 4.7. For the two-state chain of L18 slide 3 ($p_{11}=0.5$, $p_{12}=0.5$, $p_{21}=0.2$, $p_{22}=0.8$, whose steady state §3 found to be $\pi_1=2/7$, $\pi_2=5/7$), compute $t_2$ and $t_1^{*}$ and verify $\pi_1=1/t_1^{*}$.
    Solution

    $t_1=0$ and $t_2=1+p_{21}t_1+p_{22}t_2=1+0.8t_2$, so $0.2t_2=1$ and $t_2=5$ (key prac_5_t2_correct). Then $$t_1^{*}=1+p_{11}t_1+p_{12}t_2=1+0.5(0)+0.5(5)=\frac72=3.5$$ (key prac_5_t1star_correct), and $1/t_1^{*}=2/7=0.285714=\pi_1$ exactly as claimed (prac_5_pi1_correct).

    4.5 Conditioning on which absorbing state you reach

    Here is the subtlest idea in the section, and rec19(e) is built on it. Suppose you are told after the fact that the chain ended up absorbed at $s$, and you want the expected time it took. This is not $\mu_i$: $\mu_i$ averages over all absorption events, including the ones that ended somewhere else, and those may be systematically faster or slower.

    Property (conditional transition probabilities; rec19 P1(e)). Let $A$ be the event that the chain is eventually absorbed at $s$, and let $a_k=\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_0=k)$ as in §4.1. For any $i$ with $a_i>0$, $$\tilde p_{ij}\;:=\;\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=i,\,A)\;=\;\frac{a_j\,p_{ij}}{a_i} .$$ The chain conditioned on $A$ is again a Markov chain, with these transition probabilities.

    Derivation, every step. Start from the definition and apply Bayes' rule (G1 §2) with the conditioning event $\{X_n=i\}$ carried along throughout: $$\tilde p_{ij}=\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=i,A) =\frac{\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_{n+1}=j,\,X_n=i)\;\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=i)}{\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_n=i)} .$$ Now handle the three pieces. The middle factor is $p_{ij}$ by definition. The denominator is $a_i$ by definition of the absorption probability. For the numerator's first factor, $A$ is an event about the chain from time $n+1$ onward, so by the Markov property the conditioning on $X_n=i$ adds nothing once $X_{n+1}=j$ is known: $$\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_{n+1}=j,\,X_n=i)=\mathbf{P}(A\mid X_{n+1}=j)=a_j ,$$ the last step by time homogeneity. Putting the three together gives $\tilde p_{ij}=a_jp_{ij}/a_i$. $\blacksquare$

    These really are transition probabilities. Nonnegativity is clear. For the row sum, use the absorption equation $a_i=\sum_j p_{ij}a_j$ itself: $$\sum_j \tilde p_{ij}=\sum_j\frac{a_jp_{ij}}{a_i}=\frac{1}{a_i}\sum_j p_{ij}a_j=\frac{a_i}{a_i}=1 .$$ So the absorption equations are exactly the normalization condition for the conditioned chain — a pleasing piece of bookkeeping, and a useful check when you compute the $\tilde p_{ij}$ by hand.

    Interpretation — conditioning on the future re-weights the present. The factor $a_j/a_i$ is worth staring at: it says that given you know the ending, every step you take is retro-actively judged by how much it helped you get there. A move to a state $j$ that is friendlier to $s$ than your current state ($a_j>a_i$) becomes more likely than the chain's own $p_{ij}$ said; a move to a less friendly state becomes less likely; a move to a state that can never reach $s$ vanishes. This is the same phenomenon as the survivor's account of a lottery: told only about the winners, the "probability" of each choice they made looks inflated. Two practical consequences. First, it explains why the conditional expected time is not $\mu_i$: you are averaging over a different chain, not merely over a subset of runs of the old one. Second, it turns a hard question ("expected time to absorption given we ended at $s$") into a routine one: build $\tilde P$, then run the ordinary §4.2 recipe on it — no new theory, just new numbers. The recipe box of Fig. 4.4 shows exactly this as the left-hand branch's extra step.
    Gotcha — this is not a uniform renormalization. The natural but wrong instinct is: "delete the states that lead to the wrong absorbing state, then scale each row up so it sums to $1$ again". That would multiply every entry of row $i$ by the same constant. The correct weights $a_j/a_i$ depend on $j$ as well as $i$: transitions toward states that are friendly to $s$ get boosted more than transitions toward states that are barely friendly. Two consequences worth memorizing: (i) a self-transition is unchanged, $\tilde p_{ii}=a_ip_{ii}/a_i=p_{ii}$; (ii) any state $j$ with $a_j=0$ disappears entirely, $\tilde p_{ij}=0$. Both are visible in Figure 4.9, inside the part-(e) solution below (expand it to see them).
    Practice 4.8. In the chain of Fig. 4.2 ($a_1=13/28$, $a_2=5/14$, $a_3=2/7$ for target $4$), compute the conditional transition probabilities out of state $1$ given absorption at state $4$, and verify they sum to $1$.
    Solution

    With $a_4=1$, $a_2=5/14=10/28$, $a_3=2/7=8/28$ and $a_1=13/28$: $$\tilde p_{14}=\frac{a_4p_{14}}{a_1}=\frac{1\cdot0.2}{13/28}=\frac{0.2\cdot28}{13}=\frac{5.6}{13}=\frac{28}{65},$$ $$\tilde p_{12}=\frac{a_2p_{12}}{a_1}=\frac{(10/28)(0.5)}{13/28}=\frac{5}{13}=\frac{25}{65}, \qquad \tilde p_{13}=\frac{a_3p_{13}}{a_1}=\frac{(8/28)(0.3)}{13/28}=\frac{2.4}{13}=\frac{12}{65}.$$ Sum: $(28+25+12)/65=65/65=1$. Notice the arc straight into the target has been boosted from $0.2$ to $28/65=0.4308$, while the arc toward state $3$ (the state most exposed to the rival absorbing state $5$) has been cut from $0.3$ to $12/65=0.1846$ — exactly the non-uniform reweighting the Gotcha warns about.

    4.6 Recitation 19 in full: Josephina changes courses

    Example 4.6 (rec19 Problem 1 — statement). Josephina is currently a 6-1 student. On each day that she is a 6-1 student she has probability $1/2$ of being 6-1 the next day; otherwise she is equally likely to become a 6-2, 6-3, course 9 or course 15 student. On any day she is 6-3, she switches to course 9 with probability $1/4$, to 6-1 with probability $3/8$, and to 6-2 with probability $3/8$. On any day she is 6-2, she switches to course 15 with probability $1/2$, to 6-1 with probability $3/8$, and to 6-3 with probability $1/8$. She is a student forever, and for parts (a)–(f) courses 9 and 15 are permanent.

    (a) probability she eventually leaves course 6; (b) probability she eventually is in course 15; (c) expected number of days until she leaves course 6; (d) expected number of ice cream cones, where she buys one each time she switches into 6-1 from 6-2 or 6-3 but stops after two; (e) given that she ended up in course 15, the expected number of days it took; (f) with course 15 removed and 6-2's remaining probabilities kept in proportion, the expected days until course 9; (g) if courses 9 and 15 each switch to the other with probability $1/2$, the long-run distribution; (h) if courses 9 and 15 each return to 6-1 with probability $1/8$, the expected number of days until she is 6-1 again.

    Fig. 4.8 — The chain for rec19 P1(a)–(e). Reading the statement into rows: $6\text{-}1\to$ itself $1/2$ and each of $6\text{-}2$, $6\text{-}3$, $9$, $15$ with probability $\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac14=\tfrac18$; $6\text{-}2\to15$ with $1/2$, $\to6\text{-}1$ with $3/8$, $\to6\text{-}3$ with $1/8$; $6\text{-}3\to9$ with $1/4$, $\to6\text{-}1$ with $3/8$, $\to6\text{-}2$ with $3/8$. Every row sums to $1$ (checked in computes/g5_s4.py).

    Throughout, write $1=6\text{-}1$, $2=6\text{-}2$, $3=6\text{-}3$ for the transient states.

    Solution — (a) probability she eventually leaves course 6

    This is a classification question (§2), not a computation. Each of the states $6\text{-}1$, $6\text{-}2$, $6\text{-}3$ has a path to an absorbing state: $6\text{-}1\to9$ directly, $6\text{-}2\to15$ directly, $6\text{-}3\to9$ directly. Once in $9$ or $15$ there is no return, so none of the three course-6 states is accessible from $9$ or $15$. By the definition in §2 a state $i$ is transient exactly when some state is accessible from $i$ but $i$ is not accessible back — so all three course-6 states are transient. A transient state is visited only finitely many times with probability $1$, hence $$\mathbf{P}(\text{she eventually leaves course 6})=1 .$$ No equations needed. (A finite chain must end up in some recurrent class, and here the only recurrent classes are $\{9\}$ and $\{15\}$.)

    Solution — (b) probability she ends up in course 15 (the recitation shows only the answer; here is the full elimination)

    Take the target $s=15$, so $a_{15}=1$ and $a_9=0$, and write one equation per transient state using $a_i=\sum_jp_{ij}a_j$: $$a_1=\tfrac12a_1+\tfrac18(1)+\tfrac18a_2+\tfrac18(0)+\tfrac18a_3,$$ $$a_2=\tfrac12(1)+\tfrac38a_1+\tfrac18a_3,$$ $$a_3=\tfrac14(0)+\tfrac38a_1+\tfrac38a_2 .$$ Clear the denominators. Multiply each by $8$ and, in the first, move the $\tfrac12a_1$ across: $$8a_1=4a_1+1+a_2+a_3\;\Longrightarrow\;4a_1=1+a_2+a_3 \tag{I}$$ $$8a_2=4+3a_1+a_3 \tag{II}$$ $$8a_3=3a_1+3a_2 \tag{III}$$ Eliminate $a_3$. From (II), $a_3=8a_2-4-3a_1$. Substitute into (III): $$8(8a_2-4-3a_1)=3a_1+3a_2 \;\Longrightarrow\;64a_2-32-24a_1=3a_1+3a_2 \;\Longrightarrow\;61a_2=32+27a_1 ,$$ so $a_2=\dfrac{32+27a_1}{61}$. Then $$a_3=8\cdot\frac{32+27a_1}{61}-4-3a_1=\frac{256+216a_1-244-183a_1}{61}=\frac{12+33a_1}{61}.$$ Close the loop. Put both into (I): $$4a_1=1+\frac{(32+27a_1)+(12+33a_1)}{61}=1+\frac{44+60a_1}{61}.$$ Multiply by $61$: $244a_1=61+44+60a_1$, so $184a_1=105$ and $$a_1=\frac{105}{184}=0.570652 .$$ Back-substitute. $$a_2=\frac{32+27\cdot\frac{105}{184}}{61}=\frac{\frac{5888+2835}{184}}{61}=\frac{8723}{11224}=\frac{143}{184}=0.777174,$$ $$a_3=\frac{12+33\cdot\frac{105}{184}}{61}=\frac{\frac{2208+3465}{184}}{61}=\frac{5673}{11224}=\frac{93}{184}=0.505435 .$$ (The recitation quotes $0.77717$ and $0.50543$; the reduced fractions $143/184$ and $93/184$ are the same numbers — $11224=184\cdot61$. Keys rec19_a1, rec19_a2, rec19_a3; a $400{,}000$-run simulation from $6\text{-}1$ ends in course $15$ a fraction $0.570507$ of the time, rec19_a1_mc.)

    Sense check. $a_2$ is largest — from $6\text{-}2$ there is a direct $1/2$ arc into course 15. $a_3$ is smallest — $6\text{-}3$ is the only state with a $1/4$ arc into course 9 and no arc into 15. And starting at $6\text{-}1$, whose exits to $9$ and $15$ are symmetric, the answer $0.5707$ exceeds $1/2$ only because the indirect routes favor 15.

    Solution — (c) expected number of days until she leaves course 6

    Now $\mu_9=\mu_{15}=0$ and, by §4.2, $\mu_i=1+\sum_jp_{ij}\mu_j$: $$\mu_1=1+\tfrac12\mu_1+\tfrac18\mu_2+\tfrac18\mu_3,\qquad \mu_2=1+\tfrac38\mu_1+\tfrac18\mu_3,\qquad \mu_3=1+\tfrac38\mu_1+\tfrac38\mu_2 .$$ Clear denominators exactly as in (b): $$4\mu_1=8+\mu_2+\mu_3,\qquad 8\mu_2=8+3\mu_1+\mu_3,\qquad 8\mu_3=8+3\mu_1+3\mu_2 .$$ Note these are the equations of (b) with the constants $1,4,0$ replaced by $8,8,8$ — the same matrix, a different right-hand side, which is exactly what "$+1$ per step instead of a boundary prize" means. Eliminate $\mu_3$: from the second equation $\mu_3=8\mu_2-8-3\mu_1$; substituting into the third, $$8(8\mu_2-8-3\mu_1)=8+3\mu_1+3\mu_2\;\Longrightarrow\;64\mu_2-64-24\mu_1=8+3\mu_1+3\mu_2 \;\Longrightarrow\;61\mu_2=72+27\mu_1 ,$$ so $\mu_2=\dfrac{72+27\mu_1}{61}$ and $\mu_3=\dfrac{576+216\mu_1-488-183\mu_1}{61}=\dfrac{88+33\mu_1}{61}$. Close the loop: $$4\mu_1=8+\frac{(72+27\mu_1)+(88+33\mu_1)}{61}=8+\frac{160+60\mu_1}{61},$$ so $244\mu_1=488+160+60\mu_1$, i.e. $184\mu_1=648$ and $$\mu_1=\frac{648}{184}=\frac{81}{23}=3.521739$$ (the recitation writes it as $162/46$, the same number). Back-substituting, $$\mu_2=\frac{72+27\cdot\frac{81}{23}}{61}=\frac{\frac{1656+2187}{23}}{61}=\frac{3843}{1403}=\frac{63}{23}=2.739130,$$ $$\mu_3=\frac{88+33\cdot\frac{81}{23}}{61}=\frac{\frac{2024+2673}{23}}{61}=\frac{4697}{1403}=\frac{77}{23}=3.347826 .$$ (Keys rec19_mu1, rec19_mu2, rec19_mu3; simulation gives $3.520145$ days from $6\text{-}1$.) About three and a half days — short, because from $6\text{-}1$ the per-day escape probability is already $1/4$.

    Solution — (d) expected number of ice cream cones (both linear systems solved)

    Setting up. A cone is bought on every transition $6\text{-}2\to6\text{-}1$ or $6\text{-}3\to6\text{-}1$, and she stops after two. Let $N\in\{0,1,2\}$ be the number of cones bought before she leaves course 6. Define, for $j=0,1$, $$v_i(j)=\mathbf{P}\big(\text{exactly } j \text{ further such transitions occur}\mid \text{now at } i\big).$$ The word "further" is what makes the recursion work: $v_i(j)$ counts transitions from now on, so after one step the count needed drops by $1$ if the step itself was a cone-buying transition. Then $$\mathbb{E}[N]=0\cdot v_1(0)+1\cdot v_1(1)+2\cdot\big(1-v_1(0)-v_1(1)\big),$$ because $N$ is capped at $2$: every outcome with two or more cone-transitions contributes exactly $2$.

    System 1: no more cones, $v_i(0)$. Condition on tomorrow. From $6\text{-}1$, none of the five possible steps is itself a cone transition (cones are bought only when arriving at $6\text{-}1$ from $6\text{-}2$ or $6\text{-}3$), and reaching an absorbing state ends the story with no further cones, so $v_9(0)=v_{15}(0)=1$: $$v_1(0)=\tfrac12v_1(0)+\tfrac18v_2(0)+\tfrac18v_3(0)+\tfrac18(1)+\tfrac18(1).$$ From $6\text{-}2$, the step to $6\text{-}1$ (probability $3/8$) is a cone transition, so it contributes $0$ to the event "no further cones": $$v_2(0)=\tfrac38(0)+\tfrac18v_3(0)+\tfrac12(1).$$ From $6\text{-}3$, likewise the $3/8$ step to $6\text{-}1$ contributes $0$: $$v_3(0)=\tfrac38(0)+\tfrac38v_2(0)+\tfrac14(1).$$ Solve. Substitute the third into the second: $$v_2(0)=\tfrac12+\tfrac18\left(\tfrac14+\tfrac38v_2(0)\right)=\tfrac12+\tfrac1{32}+\tfrac{3}{64}v_2(0),$$ so $\left(1-\tfrac3{64}\right)v_2(0)=\tfrac{17}{32}$, i.e. $\tfrac{61}{64}v_2(0)=\tfrac{17}{32}$ and $$v_2(0)=\frac{17}{32}\cdot\frac{64}{61}=\frac{34}{61}=0.557377 .$$ Then $v_3(0)=\tfrac14+\tfrac38\cdot\tfrac{34}{61}=\tfrac14+\tfrac{51}{244}=\tfrac{61+51}{244} =\tfrac{112}{244}=\tfrac{28}{61}=0.459016$. Finally, from the first equation, $$\tfrac12v_1(0)=\tfrac14+\tfrac18\left(\tfrac{34}{61}+\tfrac{28}{61}\right) =\tfrac14+\tfrac18\cdot\tfrac{62}{61}=\tfrac14+\tfrac{31}{244}=\tfrac{61+31}{244}=\tfrac{92}{244}=\tfrac{23}{61},$$ so $v_1(0)=\dfrac{46}{61}=0.754098$ (keys rec19_v1_0, rec19_v2_0, rec19_v3_0).

    System 2: exactly one more cone, $v_i(1)$. Same conditioning, but now an absorbing state gives $0$ (she can buy no more cones once absorbed, so "exactly one more" is impossible), and a cone-buying step must be followed by no further cones — which is why $v_1(0)$ appears on the right: $$v_1(1)=\tfrac12v_1(1)+\tfrac18v_2(1)+\tfrac18v_3(1)+\tfrac18(0)+\tfrac18(0),$$ $$v_2(1)=\tfrac38\,v_1(0)+\tfrac18v_3(1)+\tfrac12(0),$$ $$v_3(1)=\tfrac38\,v_1(0)+\tfrac38v_2(1)+\tfrac14(0).$$ With $v_1(0)=\tfrac{46}{61}$ the constant is $\tfrac38\cdot\tfrac{46}{61}=\tfrac{138}{488} =\tfrac{69}{244}$. Substituting the third equation into the second: $$v_2(1)=\frac{69}{244}+\frac18\left(\frac{69}{244}+\frac38v_2(1)\right) =\frac{69}{244}+\frac{69}{1952}+\frac{3}{64}v_2(1).$$ Since $\tfrac{69}{244}=\tfrac{552}{1952}$, the constant is $\tfrac{621}{1952}$, and $\tfrac{61}{64}v_2(1)=\tfrac{621}{1952}$ gives, using $1952=61\cdot32$, $$v_2(1)=\frac{621}{1952}\cdot\frac{64}{61}=\frac{621\cdot2}{61^2}=\frac{1242}{3721}=0.333781 .$$ Then $$v_3(1)=\frac{69}{244}+\frac38\cdot\frac{1242}{3721}=\frac{4209}{14884}+\frac{1863}{14884} =\frac{6072}{14884}=\frac{1518}{3721}=0.407955 ,$$ using $14884=4\cdot3721$. Finally $$\tfrac12v_1(1)=\tfrac18\big(v_2(1)+v_3(1)\big)=\tfrac18\cdot\frac{1242+1518}{3721} =\tfrac18\cdot\frac{2760}{3721}=\frac{345}{3721},$$ so $v_1(1)=\dfrac{690}{3721}=0.185434$ (keys rec19_v1_1, rec19_v2_1, rec19_v3_1).

    Assemble. Put both on the denominator $3721=61^2$: since $v_1(0)=\tfrac{46}{61} =\tfrac{46\cdot61}{3721}=\tfrac{2806}{3721}$, $$1-v_1(0)-v_1(1)=\frac{3721-2806-690}{3721}=\frac{225}{3721}=0.060468$$ (the recitation states $225/3721$ without showing this subtraction; key rec19_v1_2plus). Therefore $$\mathbb{E}[N]=0\cdot\frac{2806}{3721}+1\cdot\frac{690}{3721}+2\cdot\frac{225}{3721} =\frac{690+450}{3721}=\frac{1140}{3721}=0.306369$$ (key rec19_EN; a $300{,}000$-run simulation gives $0.308090$, rec19_EN_mc). [SOURCE NOTE: the recitation's final display prints the middle term as $(1)v_{6\text{-}1}(0)$, but the substitution that follows uses $690/3721=v_{6\text{-}1}(1)$; the first display of part (d) has it right. We use $v_1(1)$.]

    Interpretation. Three quarters of the time she buys no cone at all, because she typically leaves course 6 within three or four days and each day she is at $6\text{-}1$ she is more likely to stay or leave than to make the round trip out and back. The cap at two cones barely matters — it only bites on $6\%$ of trajectories.

    Solution — (e) expected time to absorption, given that she ends in course 15

    The conditioned chain. By §4.5, given the event $A=\{\text{absorbed at }15\}$ the chain is Markov with $\tilde p_{ij}=a_jp_{ij}/a_i$. From part (b), and putting everything over the common denominator $184$, $$a_1=\frac{105}{184},\qquad a_2=\frac{143}{184},\qquad a_3=\frac{93}{184},\qquad a_{15}=1,\qquad a_9=0 .$$ The ratios are therefore ratios of the integers $105$, $143$, $93$ (and $184$ for state 15). Row by row: $$\tilde p_{11}=p_{11}=\tfrac12,\qquad \tilde p_{12}=\frac{143}{105}\cdot\frac18=\frac{143}{840},\qquad \tilde p_{13}=\frac{93}{105}\cdot\frac18=\frac{93}{840}=\frac{31}{280},\qquad \tilde p_{1,15}=\frac{184}{105}\cdot\frac18=\frac{23}{105};$$ $$\tilde p_{21}=\frac{105}{143}\cdot\frac38=\frac{315}{1144},\qquad \tilde p_{23}=\frac{93}{143}\cdot\frac18=\frac{93}{1144},\qquad \tilde p_{2,15}=\frac{184}{143}\cdot\frac12=\frac{92}{143};$$ $$\tilde p_{31}=\frac{105}{93}\cdot\frac38=\frac{315}{744}=\frac{105}{248},\qquad \tilde p_{32}=\frac{143}{93}\cdot\frac38=\frac{429}{744}=\frac{143}{248},\qquad \tilde p_{3,9}=\frac{a_9\,p_{3,9}}{a_3}=\frac{0\cdot\frac14}{93/184}=0 .$$ All three rows sum to $1$ (verified exactly in computes/g5_s4.py), as §4.5 promised, and course 9 has vanished from the chain because $a_9=0$.

    Fig. 4.9 — The conditioned chain of rec19 P1(e). Compare with Fig. 4.8: the self-loop at $6\text{-}1$ is unchanged at $1/2$, the arc $6\text{-}1\to15$ has grown from $1/8$ to $23/105=0.2190$, and the two arcs into course 9 are gone. This is the non-uniform reweighting of the Gotcha in §4.5.

    The new expected-time system.
